BUILDING R.O.B. - COMPLETE FIELD MANUAL


BUILDING R.O.B.
A historical engineering field manual: fabrication, circuits, firmware, perception, identity,
control, and operations




THE EVIDENCE, METHODS, FAILURES, AND FUTURE DOCUMENTATION OF ONE HAND-BUILT ROBOT
Rodolfo Aramayo / OrbitusRobotics LLC -   First Maker Faire Edition
An honest build record
Complete in scope, intentionally incomplete where the archive cannot prove the detail.

This manual connects ROB’s 95-page design presentation, private construction-photo archive, Arduino base
firmware, Cerebro v1-v5 software archive, and current Mac, iPhone, Watch, Vision Pro, lidar/map, three-camera, se-
rial, MLX/VLM, Gemini, face-identity, Messages, recording, stage-show, arm, autonomy, H.264, and secure-control
source trees.
It is an evidence-based historical reference and educational field manual, not a stamped mechanical design, pro-
duction wiring schematic, safety certification, dimensionally complete construction plan, or permission to energize
the machine. ROB was built and revised largely by hand, beginning with wood prototypes; as-built CAD does not
exist. The book preserves what the archive and author can establish, labels what was not recorded, and treats a
future measured CAD reconstruction as a new engineering project rather than silently inventing dimensions.




                           Original illustrated portrait inspired by ROB; it is visual storytelling, not engineering evidence.




Copyright © 2026 OrbitusRobotics LLC. All rights reserved. Rodolfo Aramayo is the author and photographer. Photographs are
from the private ROB build archive unless otherwise noted. Generated covers, frontispieces, story scenes, and conceptual teach-
ing plates are original project illustrations derived from or inspired by ROB reference photographs. They are illustrations, not
documentary photographs, technical drawings, or evidence of the as-built configuration. Source-code licenses remain separate
and repository-specific. This independent educational project is not affiliated with or endorsed by any film studio or franchise
owner. Product and company names remain the property of their respective owners.

First evidence-based draft: 2 August 2026. This historical engineering edition distinguishes documented facts, builder recollec-
tion, experiments, plans, and facts not established by the available evidence.
BUILDING R.O.B.                                                                                        COMPLETE FIELD MANUAL - ADVANCED



Evidence-status legend
    IMPLEMENTED                                Confirmed in the inspected current source or directly visible hardware evidence.
    COMMANDED                                  Software sends a value, but this review did not measure the physical outcome.
    EXPERIMENTAL / OPTIONAL                    Present behind a gate, dependent on external hardware, or still under active de-
                                               velopment.
    LEGACY                                     Retained compatibility or historical behavior, not the preferred new design.
    PLANNED                                    Described in a slide, TODO, protocol, or interface without proof of completed
                                               execution.
    NOT ESTABLISHED                            The available evidence does not establish the value, configuration, or physical
                                               result.

    NON-NEGOTIABLE SAFETY BOUNDARY

    ROB combines large batteries, high-current brushless motors, chains, treads, flippers, linear motion, long arms,
    machine-tool fabrication, networking, cameras, and software-controlled movement. Qualified adults must
    own the hazard analysis, power protection, emergency-stop architecture, workholding, guarding, lifting, test
    perimeter, and public operation. Never infer a safe circuit or fabrication dimension from a photograph. Never
    treat an ordinary IR sensor, software timeout, or cloud model as safety-rated protective equipment.


   ONE CURRENT ARDUINO, THREE HISTORICAL SKETCHES

   The ROBArduino archive preserves Base, Torso, and Head sketches from an earlier three-Arduino design. The
   builder reports that present-day ROB uses only the Base sketch. This book therefore treats Base behavior
   as the current low-level firmware reference and labels Torso and Head behavior as retired historical evidence.
   Source files cannot prove which binary is flashed today; record the board identity, firmware hash, build envi-
   ronment, and upload date before operation.


   TWO AMBER B1 ARMS, ONE DOCUMENTED CONTROL CHAIN

   ROB carries two seven-joint AMBER B1 arms. The repository snapshot separates their Ubuntu-side cores as
   L-10 and R-11: network clients use UDP or LCM, each core binds a stable CAN interface, and CAN carries com-
   mands and feedback between that core and its arm actuators. The AmberHomeFolder tree is evidence from one
   Ubuntu machine, not a deployable image; never copy its credentials, logs, virtual environment, or machine-
   specific identifiers. Volume 6 documents the inspected protocol, URDF models, reproducible setup, verifica-
   tion gates, and unresolved configuration differences.


   HOW TO FIND THE FILES

   Open the public repositories on GitHub and follow each printed repository-relative path: https://github.com
   /RudyAramayo/ROBBooks, https://github.com/RudyAramayo/ROBArduino, https://github.com/RudyAramayo/C
   erebro, https://github.com/RudyAramayo/ROBController, https://github.com/RudyAramayo/ROBController
   Vision, https://github.com/RudyAramayo/Amber-HomeFolder, and https://github.com/RudyAramayo/ROBTrai
   ningGames. The website is published separately at https://github.com/OrbitusRoboticsWebSite/ORobotics.
   See https://github.com/RudyAramayo/ROBBooks/blob/main/ROB-Books/OPEN-SOURCE-CODE-MAP.md for clickable
   repository and file links, license cautions, and renamed-file search instructions. A named archival item without
   a verified public repository is labeled as evidence, not given an invented URL.




Source snapshot and publication cautions
The initial evidence pass began on 2 August 2026; this expanded hardware, AMBER, model-training, iden-
tity/privacy, mapping, and three-feed-video edition reflects the repositories inspected through 23 August 2026,

ORBITUS ROBOTICS - FIRST MAKER FAIRE EDITION                                                                                  3 / 220
BUILDING R.O.B.                                                                           COMPLETE FIELD MANUAL - ADVANCED


the builder interview of 21 August, and follow-up author answers received 29 August. ROBControllerVision’s latest
three panorama-orientation commits were still ahead of its public remote. Cross-repository publication still requires
an intentional tagged/tested release. Original gallery photographs were converted with orientation applied and
metadata stripped. Attendee photographs, especially images containing children, require release review or careful
exclusion.
The archived B-COMMAND drawings prohibit reproduction without permission, and the HengDrive drawings are
marked proprietary/confidential. The public edition neither reproduces those records nor derives a construction
specification from them. It keeps only author recollection, facts visible on builder-owned hardware, and authorized
public-source information. A future technical edition may use the private records only after written permission or
an authorized public replacement datasheet is obtained.

         Part I

Intent and architecture




           1
CHAPTER 1


The robot as a long-running
experiment
CHAPTER 1


Design history and current
scope
ROB is not one frozen machine. The archive shows a succession of working prototypes, public demonstrations,
repair decisions, new sensors, drivetrain modifications, camera changes, arm work, and software migrations. A
useful build manual must preserve that timeline because the answer to “How is it built?” depends on the date and
subsystem.



The one hard constraint
The tracked era began in College Station, Texas, with the purchase of the SuperDroid Robots LT2 platform; the
builder dates that new treaded prototype to approximately 2016. The tread system was the one interface he chose
not to redesign. Everything else—body width, battery placement, drivetrain packaging, flipper geometry, service
openings, cable paths, and eventually the upper robot—had to negotiate with it.
The first design instrument was cardboard. The builder mocked the LT2 envelope at full scale, moved imagined
components until they fit, and used successful faces as templates for maple plywood. A third-wheel/flipper idea
drew inspiration from Johnny 5 in Short Circuit: a perpendicular wheel might lift part of the contact patch and reduce
the sideways scrub required for a heavy tracked robot to turn. This is not merely a charming origin story. It is the
repeatable method behind ROB:

1. freeze the purchased interface and record its revision;
2. make a cheap full-size representation;
3. write one question on each prototype;
4. test packaging, reach, sweep, balance, access, and cable bend before strength;
5. transfer only proven geometry into plywood or metal;
6. preserve failed versions and the decision they changed.

  ADVANCED CHANNEL

  A cardboard prototype proves geometry only within its scale and stiffness. It cannot certify structural strength,
  fastener retention, tread tension, electrical insulation, thermal behavior, payload, stopping distance, or public
  safety. Record both what each mockup proved and what it could not prove.

Cerebro's recovered software roots
The current public Cerebro repository begins on 5 August 2025 with a commit that calls itself an initial v5 change in a
fresh repository. The preserved v1-v5 workshop folders show that this is a migration boundary rather than Cerebro’s
beginning.
The pre-Git v1 folder retains an upstream Cocoa Arduino serial example dated 2009. That date belongs to the
starting example, not ROB. ROB-specific headers and project state date the adaptation to 2017-2018: the Mac
application is named Cerebro, opens a 250000-baud USB serial path, and issues forward, reverse, turn, flipper, and
linear-actuator commands in ROB’s seven-field text frame. Keyboard and multipeer scaffolding show the command
station beginning to accept additional operator inputs.
The first surviving Cerebro Git history starts with 0141a646 on 1 January 2018. During 2018, v2 adds speech, separate

ORBITUS ROBOTICS - FIRST MAKER FAIRE EDITION                                                                                                      4 / 220
BUILDING R.O.B.                                                                           COMPLETE FIELD MANUAL - ADVANCED


Base/Head/Torso serial paths, bound Cocoa windows, SceneKit and multipeer controllers, OpenNI/PCL/NiTE point-
cloud and user-tracking experiments, a named consciousness bridge, and Leap Motion. The later v3 archive records
controller arbitration and autonomy permission, Maestro servos, multilingual speech, ReSpeaker and RealSense task
processes, human/head tracking, RTSP, T265, performance work, AutoNet, and the 2022 RPLidar repository split.
A related v4 branch carries the old 2018 root through Apple-silicon, USB/network, Google-LLM, singleton-process,
continuous-speech, and Foundation Models work in July 2025.
The v5 root 4c4f1d4 has no parent, but its imported tree already contains the accumulated robot program. Preserve
the old Git stores and file fingerprints separately: v3 and v4 are related branches rather than one invented straight
line, and third-party examples and SDKs retain their own attribution and licensing. The detailed archive record is
ROB-Books/CEREBRO_ARCHIVE_HISTORY.md.



Design principles that recur
Across the sources, several durable principles emerge:

• use prototypes to discover packaging and load problems;
• separate high-level intent from low-level actuator values;
• keep independent stop layers close to each source of failure;
• isolate optional cameras, Python helpers, video, speech, and AI from core motion control;
• preserve service access and label wiring;
• make unsupported capability fail honestly;
• collect evidence before calling a proposal implemented.

ORBITUS ROBOTICS - FIRST MAKER FAIRE EDITION                                                                                         6 / 220

   THE BUILDER'S ACCOUNT

   ROB is a friendly name derived from “robot,” not an acronym. Rodolfo Aramayo began the project after leaving
   Verizon Labs, where he had worked as a systems software engineer on mobile VR/AR research technologies.
   He recalls building ROB in College Station, Texas, and dates the new treaded prototype to approximately 2016;
   no exact month is asserted. The surviving 2017–2018 Cerebro serial-control work belongs to the first physical
   prototype.
   The ambition was to bring the friendly, expressive idea of Johnny 5 into an era of mobile interfaces and 3D
   depth cameras. That film character is historical inspiration only; ROB’s design, artwork, name, and implemen-
   tation are independent. Early Cerebro code also called a local CakeChat neural-dialogue service. The source
   proves an early pre-ChatGPT conversational experiment, not that CakeChat was a technical ancestor of Chat-
   GPT.
   Aramayo identifies himself as ROB’s sole builder, author, and principal software developer. Apple, Arashi
   Vision/Insta360, HengDrive, B-COMMAND, NETGEAR, AMBER Robotics, SuperDroid Robots, Arduino, and
   SLAMTEC/SLAMWARE supplied platforms or components; they are not presented as co-builders, sponsors,
   or endorsers.
   The first era combined a car battery, Power Wheels hardware, scrap wood, an older Intel laptop, and an old
   robot arm. The LT2 tracked era added a third-wheel/flipper experiment, then exposed an underpowered
   roughly 30 W motor choice. Gear changes and a later 12-to-24 V boost stage improved torque and speed
   without making ROB fast. Later eras added lidar, a stepper torso, Maestro-controlled hobby servos, and then
   heavy AMBER B1 arms. Years of difficulty coordinating the first arm arrangement eventually led to a dedicated
   dual-arm system purchased for approximately $22,500. The current era uses AI-assisted development to ex-
   pand secure mobile and Vision Pro control, three-camera H.264 viewing, depth processing, machine vision,
   speech, and bounded model integrations.
   Official Maker Faire records identify R.O.B. as a Bay Area exhibit in 2019 at the San Mateo County Event Center
   in San Mateo, and again in 2023, 2024, and 2025 at Mare Island in Vallejo. The fairs ran May 17–19, 2019;
   October 13–15 and 20–22, 2023; October 18–20, 2024; and September 26–28, 2025. The September 2026
   fair is still in the future as of this August record, so no 2026 appearance is claimed.




     A 2019 side view records the tracked base, compact body, early head, exposed mechanisms, and hobby-servo arm era.
     It is one revision in ROB’s evolution rather than a final reproducible configuration.

CHAPTER 2


System boundaries and
ownership
CHAPTER 2


Whole-robot architecture
                                          Operator intent - iPhone, Apple Watch, or Vision Pro controller




                             Authenticated control session - pairing, TLS/QUIC, roles, sequence, applicable freshness




                                     Cerebro on macOS - UI, coordination, cameras, speech, autonomy gates




                                                 USB serial heartbeat - fixed base command frame




                                        Arduino base controller - PWM, direction, brakes, actuator, IMU, IR




                          Physical plant - treads, flippers, linear actuator (installed role to verify), battery and mechanics




Physical and information paths
The robot contains two overlapping architectures:

1. The energy architecture moves stored electrical energy through protection, conversion, controllers, conductors,
   and motors.
2. The information architecture moves intent and measurements through interfaces, authentication, state ma-
   chines, protocols, adapters, firmware, and feedback.

The two paths meet at motor controllers and actuators. Keeping their documentation separate prevents a common
error: assuming that a correct data value proves the high-current circuit is safe, or that a correctly wired motor proves
the command path is authorized.



Desired component ownership map
The table below states the target responsibility split. It is a design contract, not a claim that every current imple-
mentation already satisfies every responsibility; gaps must remain visible in the subsystem status and verification
chapters.

                                               camera + IR + IMU           Mac mini + apps               motors + arms



                                                                   feedback changes the next choice



Manual control, deterministic autonomy, and AI-assisted behavior can all use the same physical plant, but they
should enter through explicit authority gates. EXPERIMENTAL / OPTIONAL Current social-roam autonomy consumes fresh
bounded lidar scans and, near 82 percent of its configured activation radius, attempts to turn and drive toward its
origin. Destination autonomy adds bounded routing and further stale-sensor gates; neither behavior is a physical
geofence or proof of containment. Optional Gemini tools declare high-level action proposals, but most do not yet
have executors. None of these paths should bypass local validation, the control-master state, the Arduino deadline,
or the physical emergency stop.

   ADVANCED CHANNEL

   Document safety as a control hierarchy, not a list of features. For every hazard, name the prevention, detection,
   mitigation, independent stop, diagnostic coverage, proof test, and residual risk. Ordinary cameras, IR sensors,
   and software timers may support risk reduction but are not automatically safety-rated.


   RECORD LIMIT: NOT AN AS-BUILT BLOCK DIAGRAM

   The source and photographs support the functional ownership diagram in this chapter, but no dated, author-
   approved as-built drawing of every present power, data, and external port was preserved. Historical Raspberry
   Pi concepts, retired Arduino roles, and earlier arm electronics must not be blended into the current M4 Mac
   mini, Mega 2560, Maestro, Tic, AMBER Ubuntu, depth-camera, panoramic-camera, lidar, and network account.
   Every diagram in this edition is therefore architectural teaching material. Reproduction requires a new physical
   inventory and traced schematic.



ORBITUS ROBOTICS - FIRST MAKER FAIRE EDITION                                                                                                  10 / 220
     Part II

Mechanical build




       11
CHAPTER 3


Chassis, treads, and drivetrain
CHAPTER 3


Build the mobile base
The base is the foundation for every later system. Presentation pages 3-9 show left and right wooden side templates,
motor openings, wheel mounts, a battery opening, and the desire to reproduce the form in black powder-coated alu-
minum. Pages 10-20 document the tread tensioner, wheels, sprockets, and shaft collars. Later gallery photographs
show extensive metal and wiring revisions.
The purchased SuperDroid Robots LT2 system is the governing mechanical interface. The vendor’s current support
material describes the LT2 as a mid-sized, chain-driven skid-steer platform and explains that flipper arms can lift the
nose, stabilize the platform, help it climb, and right it after a rollover. Current catalog dimensions and options may
not match ROB’s purchased revision; retain the invoice, serial/revision evidence, and as-received measurements
with the CAD.



Start from interfaces
Before drawing the final plate, freeze or explicitly version these interfaces:

• tread-frame mounting points and full motion envelope;
• drive and idler shaft positions, diameters, retention, and bearing supports;
• motor face, shaft, and cable clearances;
• chain line, sprocket sizes, guard volume, and service access;
• flipper shaft, torque path, rear/front location, and end stops;
• battery envelope, restraint, protection access, and removal path;
• actuator mounts and full extension/retraction envelope;
• lifting, tie-down, stand, cover, and inspection points.




                                                                                                                        A later sprocket view shows why
A centered rear view shows the drive assembly between two treads.        chain-line and plate clearance need dimensioned control.




Tread tension and support
                                                                    13
BUILDING R.O.B.                                                                                    COMPLETE FIELD MANUAL - ADVANCED



The presentation describes aluminum bars with threaded holes that pull the tread assembly outward, free-spinning
wheels retained on shafts, and a heavy-load problem that led to improvised HDPE and skateboard-bearing supports.
These are valuable design clues but not a final service specification.
Record a repeatable tension method. A statement such as “tight enough” cannot be audited. Depending on the
tread system, the specification might be deflection under a known force, tensioner position, exposed thread length,
torque plus verification, or the manufacturer’s procedure.




                                                                                                         The close chain path illustrates
A large sprocket and chain are visible inside the base.    alignment, abrasion, and guarding concerns.



    SAFETY CHECK

    Chains, sprockets, and treads create drawing-in, nip, shear, and entanglement hazards. Disconnect and verify
    power, block stored motion, prevent unintended rolling, and use fixed guards before hands enter the mecha-
    nism.



Flipper revision and third-wheel concept
Presentation pages 21-26 say the flippers moved from the front wheels to the rear after a design revision. The deck
also records a one-degree bar offset, cotter-pin concerns, and a proposed perpendicular third wheel raised by an
actuator to reduce tread scrub while turning.




ORBITUS ROBOTICS - FIRST MAKER FAIRE EDITION                                                                                14 / 220
BUILDING R.O.B.                                                                                 COMPLETE FIELD MANUAL - ADVANCED




     The annotated upside-down prototype on presentation page 23 captures the third-wheel idea and identifies a design
     revision. It is not a final drawing.

  PLANNED   Treat the third wheel as a separate design project until the builder supplies the geometry, load path,
stability analysis, actuator selection, ground clearance, deployed/retracted sensing, failure response, and test evi-
dence.
The builder’s intended stair technique was an undulating sequence: a high-torque flipper lifts part of ROB, the treads
advance, and the body changes support state before the next step. Added arms, cameras, computers, inverter,
batteries, and structure increased normal force and tread scrub. The present robot can be difficult to pivot on
concrete and may need small forward/back steering arcs. Reproduce neither stair climbing nor a zero-radius turn
until the test plan includes support polygon, tip margin, flipper current/temperature, tread traction, tread-to-step
engagement, cable clearance, observer exclusion, and an independent stop.



Drivetrain experiment ledger
The builder recalls an early combination operating the motors from 12 V at roughly 80 rpm and 30 W, then tri-
als involving 16-, 32-, 40-, and 45-tooth sprockets. The photographs and public records do not establish which
winding, gearbox stage, controller setting, supply condition, or motor variant was installed during each trial. The
recollections remain historical clues until the installed units and revision dates are identified.
For every revision, record both sprockets and which one drives. If Nd is driver teeth and No is output teeth, the ideal
output-speed multiplier is Nd /No and the ideal torque multiplier is No /Nd before losses. A larger isolated tooth
count does not tell which outcome occurred.
  REV                   DRIVER                 DRIVEN    BUS        MEASURE AND RECORD
  Historical            16?                    unknown   12 V?      No-load shaft/tread speed, current, controller set-
                                                                    ting, surface, mass, and temperature.
  Trial                 32?                    unknown   12/24 V?   Identify whether speed increased by ratio, voltage, or
                                                                    both.
  Trial                 40?                    unknown   12/24 V?   Measure turning current and flipper lift margin.
  Trial                 45?                    unknown   12/24 V?   Record chain line, wrap, tension, guard clearance,
                                                                    and failure reason.

ORBITUS ROBOTICS - FIRST MAKER FAIRE EDITION                                                                         15 / 220
 BUILDING R.O.B.                                                                           COMPLETE FIELD MANUAL - ADVANCED


 The later 12-to-24 V boost produced an immediate observed improvement in speed and available effort. Voltage
 strongly affects a DC motor’s available speed; torque is primarily current-dependent. Do not publish “doubling
 voltage doubled torque” as a general law. Measure bus voltage at the controller, phase/input current as appropriate,
 converter droop, controller current limit, output speed, force, and temperature under the same load.



 Provisional assembly sequence to verify
 1. inspect plate dimensions, datum features, threads, deburring, and finish;
 2. install bearings, shaft supports, and fixed structural brackets;
 3. trial-fit tread frames, drive motors, shafts, sprockets, and chains without power;
 4. measure shaft parallelism, sprocket coplanarity, tension range, and full-motion clearance;
 5. install flipper shaft and drivetrain with temporary manual limits;
 6. fit actuator brackets through the full stroke with the load supported independently;
 7. install guarded power components and labeled harnesses only after mechanical inspection;
 8. add batteries using a rated lift and restraint plan;
 9. perform continuity, insulation, polarity, and protective-device checks;
10. test one actuator at a time on stands under a bounded current and command limit;
11. install guards and covers before floor testing.

    RECORD LIMIT: HAND-BUILT GEOMETRY

    ROB was fitted and revised by hand from wood prototypes and later metal work. No final as-built CAD, con-
    trolled dimension set, fastener/torque schedule, guard drawing, weighed mass, or measured center of mass
    survives. The LT2 tread system identifies the purchased starting interface, but photographs cannot establish its
    revision or the geometry built around it. A future reproduction must begin with a measured survey or scan of
    the present robot and becomes a new design requiring its own load, stability, guarding, and transport analysis.




 ORBITUS ROBOTICS - FIRST MAKER FAIRE EDITION                                                                   16 / 220
CHAPTER 4


Torso, body, head, and arms
CHAPTER 4


Build above the base
The upper structure must hold cameras and arms while surviving acceleration, arm moments, cable pull, transport,
and public interaction. The presentation openly records weak prototype areas: body-to-torso joints pulling on
metal, bent L brackets, and wooden parts glued and screwed together.



Body-to-torso connection
Presentation pages 56-60 show the connection under load and call for mechanical improvement. A final design
should provide:

• a defined load path into the chassis;
• bearing surfaces or pivots sized for radial, axial, and moment loads;
• hard stops and motion limits independent of software;
• retention that remains safe after one fastener loosens, where practicable;
• inspection access and visible wear indicators;
• cable routing that does not become the motion stop;
• a transport lock that does not depend on powered holding torque.



Body rotation and cable continuity
Presentation page 67 preserves an earlier limited-rotation body-bearing concept. The builder reports a custom B-
COMMAND rotary interface installed inside the stepper plate so power and data can cross the rotating boundary.
He also reports removing an incompatible plastic piece and bonding the rotary interface into the plate. Treat that as
an as-built repair requiring adhesive identification, bond-area inspection, axial/radial retention, runout, strain relief,
thermal review, and a service/replacement plan—not as a vendor-approved mounting method.
The visible installation and the builder’s account establish the rotary link’s role, but not its public electrical or me-
chanical rating. Controlled drawings in the private archive are excluded from this edition. Record photographs of
every label and lead, identify the exact installed unit, and obtain an authorized public manufacturer record before
assigning conductor ampacity, voltage, ingress, temperature, service-life, speed, or data-interface limits. A sec-
ond custom head rotary-link idea remains PLANNED unless the installed hardware is photographed, identified, and
continuity-tested.

  SAFETY CHECK

  No component rating would by itself authorize a torso speed. Human proximity, arm sweep, cable motion,
  stepper torque, balance, stopping distance, controller voltage, and structure require a separately verified op-
  erating envelope. The exact Tic model, motor rating, configuration, present supply, and rotary-link limits must
  be identified before changing voltage or speed.

body motion can pull on a dense harness.                                       Temporary labels aid teardown, but permanent routing must account for

Head and neck
The archive spans an earlier spherical multicamera head, an Insta360 Pro II concept, OAK-D sensors, a later spherical
head, and a 2025 geared neck mechanism. The builder identifies the present panoramic camera as an Insta360 Pro
II and the real-time head depth camera as an autofocus OAK-D Pro.
Pan, lower-neck tilt, and upper-neck tilt use Hitec HSB-9380TH servos commanded by the 24-channel Pololu Mae-
stro. Hitec’s public specification identifies a 6.0–7.4 V digital brushless servo, a 25-tooth output spline, 34 kg-cm
maximum torque at 7.4 V, 0.14 s/60 degrees at 7.4 V, and approximately 2.1 A stall current. The builder recalls a
12-tooth servo gear driving a 92-tooth neck gear. That may describe an attached pinion rather than the servo spline,
but it must be measured. A 12:92 external ratio would ideally slow the neck and multiply torque by 92/12 ≈ 7.67
before losses; it would also multiply backlash and reflected structure loads in ways the servo rating alone cannot
certify.




                                               The OAK-D stereo camera is a
distinct sensing module, not the whole head design.                            The 2025 neck gear train exposes stacked gears and support plates that need
                                                                               calibrated limits and guarded pinch points.

CHAPTER 5


From template to finished part
CHAPTER 5


Fabrication and finish
The presentation asks how to print and weld the aluminum, whether templates or a 3D scan would help, and whether
an outside robotics shop should quote the metalwork. Those questions prove that a complete final fabrication
recipe is not present. This chapter supplies the record structure the builder should fill.



Measurement plan
Choose datums before collecting dimensions. For every critical measurement, record:

• feature and datum names;
• nominal value and unit;
• tolerance or functional clearance;
• instrument and resolution;
• repeated measurements and uncertainty;
• temperature or load state if relevant;
• photograph or drawing revision;
• who measured and when.




A digital caliper is used across the tread frame; the publication needs the datum        A fit check can reveal interface errors before a hole becomes permanent.
and recorded value.




Process traveler
For each fabricated part, create a traveler with these gates:

1. released drawing and material certificate or stock identification;
2. saw/cut blank with allowance;
3. workholding setup and datum pickup;


                                                                                    22
BUILDING R.O.B.                                                                            COMPLETE FIELD MANUAL - ADVANCED


 4. roughing operation;
 5. intermediate inspection;
 6. finish holes, pockets, threads, bends, or weld preparation;
 7. deburr and edge-break requirement;
 8. weld/braze/bond sequence and distortion check, if applicable;
 9. surface preparation and coating;
10. final dimensions, photographs, and acceptance signature;
11. nonconformance and rework record.

    SAFETY CHECK

   The builder or qualified shop must write the actual machine, cutter, feeds and speeds, workholding, coolant,
   chip control, guarding, ventilation, hot-work, welding, grinding, coating, and PPE instructions. A generic book
   cannot make a particular shop operation safe.




Design for assembly and service
Use captive or accessible fasteners where covers need regular removal. Separate abrasive motion and sharp edges
from harnesses. Provide inspection windows for chain line, tension, battery restraints, and protective devices. En-
sure a heavy component can be supported before its final fastener is removed. Include lifting points and a stand
plan in the CAD, not as an afterthought.

   HISTORICAL FABRICATION RECORD

   The surviving record is a sequence of hand fitting, wood templates, drilling, bracket work, machining revisions,
   coating, and assembly photographs rather than a controlled shop traveler. The author was the sole builder and
   has not supplied machine parameters, weld procedures, adhesive records, inspection results, or a complete
   cost ledger. This chapter teaches how a future team should create those records; it does not reverse-engineer
   feeds, speeds, tolerances, or acceptance criteria from photographs.

Power, electronics, and firmware

Power architecture
CHAPTER 6


Store, convert, protect, and
disconnect energy
What the archive says
The builder reports four 20 Ah LiFePO4 batteries and refers to the bank as 80 Ah. That arithmetic describes a com-
patible parallel capacity, but a safe parallel topology is not established until pack voltage, chemistry, BMS behavior,
state-of-charge matching, branch protection, conductor resistance, and manufacturer permission are verified. Later
photographs show only part of the physical arrangement and cannot prove the hidden connections.
The current narrative says all battery negative leads meet at a negative bus. Positive leads travel to the front on/off
switch and then to a positive 12 V bus bar. A large converter boosts the base supply to 24 V for the motors and
upper-body feed. Behind the belly are separate 12 V and 24 V buses. A 24-to-12 V DC converter supplies selected
body loads and the 12 V input path for the fanless sine-wave inverter. A 24-to-48 V boost stage supplies the AMBER
B1 arms through the two cutoff switches behind the arms. These statements are builder-reported and must be
reconciled against an as-built single-line drawing and continuity test.

Required single-line diagram
The final manual needs an electrical single-line diagram from every energy source to every load. At minimum,
identify:

• battery pack chemistry, nominal and full-charge voltage, capacity, BMS, and series/parallel connection;
• main fuse or breaker near each energy source;
• main disconnect and emergency-stop contactor/controller path;
• precharge, inrush, anti-spark, or safe connector sequence where required;
• 12 V, 24 V, 5 V, and other buses with converter ratings and derating;
• tread, flipper, actuator, arm, compute, sensor, light, audio, router, and accessory branches;
• wire gauge, insulation rating, connector, polarity, routing, and strain relief;
• grounding, chassis bonding, shielding, and separation of noisy and sensitive returns;
• charger interlock, shore-power protection, inverter isolation, and exposed receptacles;
• test points, indicators, and safe discharge behavior.

The onboard NOCO Genius charger is recalled as a 2 A-class unit. It is a fallback charging path, not evidence
that ROB can operate indefinitely while plugged in. At 2 A, even an ideal 80 Ah bank requires on the order of 40
hours to replace 80 Ah before taper, losses, cell balancing, and concurrent loads. Record the exact charger model
and approved battery topology. Interlock motion and hazardous charging/service states according to the electrical
design.



Motor electrical evidence
The motor label visible in build photographs identifies a HengDrive B5685G family. The public evidence does
not identify the installed winding, gearbox, controller interface, voltage range, or performance curve. Controlled
vendor records in the builder’s archive are excluded as technical sources for this public edition; no connector assign-
ment, ratio, rating, or curve is inferred from them. An authorized public manufacturer record and a non-destructive
identification test are required before any of those values becomes a specification.
The required identification test is non-destructive and staged: photograph every label and connector, record wind-
ing/gearbox/serial markings, trace each five-wire harness without energizing, compare controller configuration,
then measure bounded no-load output speed at the shaft and tread. Only after current limiting, direction, brake,
tach level, and stop are known should a loaded test record output speed, input current, converter voltage sag,
temperature, and force.

   ADVANCED CHANNEL

   A builder-recalled 240 W converter corresponds to 10 A output at 24 V before losses. The 12 V input would
   require more than 20 A at full output after conversion loss. This does not approve or reject the circuit; it
   explains why both input and output conductors, fuses, connectors, cooling, battery sag, and simultaneous
   motor demand require measurement.




Emergency stop and safe energy state

ORBITUS ROBOTICS - FIRST MAKER FAIRE EDITION                                                                                28 / 220
BUILDING R.O.B.                                                                             COMPLETE FIELD MANUAL - ADVANCED



A physical E-stop should be independent of the UI and ordinary network. Its design must follow the actual hazard
analysis: which energy is removed, which holding brakes remain, whether abrupt removal creates a worse hazard,
how reset works, how contact welding or controller failure is detected, and how the operator proves it before a
show.




     A yellow emergency-stop button and control boxes appear in a 2025 bench layout. The photograph does not reveal
     contacts, safety category, or final wiring.


    NO PUBLIC AS-BUILT POWER SCHEMATIC

    No authoritative current schematic, protection study, conductor schedule, BMS compatibility record, E-
    stop validation, or fault-test report was supplied. The photographed battery, bus, converter, charger, and
    emergency-stop components are historical evidence only. This edition deliberately withholds a construction
    power tree: copying a visible layout or remembered topology could create fire, shock, uncontrolled-motion,
    or battery hazards. A future energized build requires a traced as-built schematic and qualified electrical review
    independent of this book.

Three historical roles, one present role
The ROBArduino archive resolves an important architecture question. Earlier ROB revisions had three named Arduino
sketches, but the builder reports that present-day ROB uses only the Base code.

Current diagrams, startup procedures, and hardware counts must show one Base Arduino. Historical diagrams may
show all three only when labeled with their revision context. Cerebro still contains serial names and helpers for Base,

boards remain installed.

digital I/O pins, 15 PWM outputs, 16 analog inputs, and four hardware UARTs; the remembered “55 digital I/O”
count is corrected here. The exact board revision, core, library versions, flashed binary/hash, and upload date
remain to be supplied.

Cerebro consumes the existing WARNING! FRONT, WARNING! BACK, and blocking-error sentences without requiring a
firmware flash. Its SceneKit diagnostic highlights the corresponding pair for three seconds. Silence becomes “clear-
ance unknown,” not “path clear,” because the legacy stream has neither explicit clear events nor numeric distances.
These terrain-sensitive IR warnings do not replace RPLidar traversability or a physical stop.
The existing Base sketch emits BEGIN BASE STARTUP SEQUENCE after IMU initialization. Cerebro pulses DTR to restart
each USB serial candidate, waits up to 15 seconds for that exact role-specific line, retains the matching descrip-
tor, and reruns the scan when the operator refreshes. It transmits no command bytes and requires no firmware
flash. This removes reliance on a hub-dependent /dev/cu.* name, but it is role discovery rather than cryptographic
authentication or hardware verification.



Serial frame
        ~+0000,+0100,+0000,+0100,+0000,+0000,+0000


ORBITUS ROBOTICS - FIRST MAKER FAIRE EDITION                                                                                                           32 / 220
 BUILDING R.O.B.                                                                            COMPLETE FIELD MANUAL - ADVANCED


 The 42 ASCII bytes represent left brake, left signed speed, right brake, right signed speed, flipper brake, flipper
 signed speed, and actuator signed speed. Brake field 0 maps to HIGH and is described as released; nonzero maps
 to LOW and is described as engaged. Tread and flipper speed output is 255 - abs(command), consistent with the
 active-low motor control concept. The physical brake electronics are not documented by the five-wire motor outline.



 Actuator compact serial
 The code sends 0x83 to exit safe start, 0x85 for forward, and 0x86 for reverse. It encodes magnitude in a five-bit low
 chunk and remaining high bits. Special input values +3201 and -3201 also clear safe start before becoming +3200
 or -3200. Reset should be separated from speed in a future typed protocol.



 IR and IMU status
   EXPERIMENTAL / OPTIONAL Six IR sensors are constructed and front/back readings are sampled. After ten repeated close
 readings, the code prints a warning. The two lines that assert movement-inhibit flags are commented out, side
 sensors do not participate, and full six-value telemetry is commented. The builder reports grass/floor false triggers.
   IMPLEMENTED  MPU9250 acceleration, gyro, and magnetometer readings feed a Mahony quaternion filter and print
 yaw, pitch, roll, and temperature. The orientation is telemetry only; tilt does not inhibit motion. The yaw correction
 is hard-coded for an older Austin, Texas declination and must not be treated as current calibration.



 Safety and correctness audit
 The historical firmware should be preserved as an artifact, then replaced behind fixtures. Priority defects include:

 1. the five-byte numeric buffer lacks a C-string null terminator before atoi and debug printing;
 2. one available byte triggers 41 further reads without proof of a complete frame;
 3. separator positions, signs, ranges, checksum, and frame completeness are not validated;
 4. an extra read after the seventh field can consume the next frame’s start marker;
 5. any received byte refreshes the deadman before frame validation;
 6. the deadman depends on loop counts rather than elapsed monotonic time;
 7. timeout sends zero speed with brake fields that release brakes;
 8. IR inhibit assignments are disabled and would not update outputs until another command path runs;
 9. one rotation test duplicates the same condition and omits the mirrored turn;
10. command values are not clamped to PWM and actuator ranges;
11. ramp constants, encoder feedback, and voltage monitoring are TODO-only;
12. brake, direction, and PWM update ordering has not been proven jerk-free;
13. the flipper and actuator have no firmware-visible end stops, position feedback, or motion timeout;
14. setup writes the inactive value 255 to both tread PWM pins but never initializes flipper PWM D6; after
    pinMode(OUTPUT), its default LOW is the maximum-command level under the active-low mapping, so boot
    safety relies on the unverified flipper-brake path;
15. IMU initialization can abort before motor outputs are explicitly initialized safe;
16. direction method names, comments, and debug strings disagree.




 ORBITUS ROBOTICS - FIRST MAKER FAIRE EDITION                                                                    33 / 220
BUILDING R.O.B.                                                                                       COMPLETE FIELD MANUAL - ADVANCED


    SAFETY CHECK

    Do not “clean up” direction logic on the live robot by inspection. First capture protocol fixtures, lift and guard
    the drivetrain, limit current and speed, verify every brake and direction state, measure timeout behavior, and
    retain an immediate independent stop.




Replacement-firmware architecture
A modern base controller should contain:

• safe GPIO initialization before optional sensors;
• bounded ring buffer and explicit framed decoder;
• version, length, type, sequence, range, and checksum validation;
• atomic command acceptance only after a complete valid frame;
• a monotonic deadline refreshed only by a valid authorized command;
• explicit states: boot-safe, disarmed, armed-neutral, moving, stopping, fault, E-stop;
• separate commands for reset, arm, drive, flipper, actuator, and diagnostic requests;
• clamped outputs, ramp and direction-change interlocks, and defined brake/coast policy;
• end-stop, encoder, current, voltage, and temperature feedback where hardware supports it;
• structured telemetry with units and status flags;
• unit, parser-fuzz, timing, and hardware-in-the-loop tests.



Interrupt-first rewire proposal
The motor controllers expose speed feedback, but the installed code never attaches interrupts. The original map
spends external-interrupt-capable D2 and D3 on right-motor outputs, proposes D20 for one tach while I2 C uses
D20/D21, and uses SoftwareSerial despite spare hardware UARTs. The following is a design proposal, not autho-
rization to move wires:
  FUNCTION                                PROPOSED           MIGRATION NOTE
                                          MEGA PIN
  Right / left / flipper tach             D18 / D19 / D2     All external interrupts;      verify voltage, pull-up, edge,
                                                             pulses/revolution, noise, and maximum rate.
  Right speed / brake / direc-            D8 / D24 / D25     D8 retains PWM; D24 is ordinary digital output. Prove the con-
  tion                                                       troller’s active-low speed behavior.
  Left speed / brake / direc-             D4 / D5 / D27      Preserve current assignments.
  tion
  Flipper speed / brake / di-             D6 / D7 / D29      Preserve current assignments and add independent end/position
  rection                                                    protection.
  LACT controller                         Serial2 D16 TX /   Replace SoftwareSerial; connect only the required direction(s)
                                          D17 RX             with verified 5 V/3.3 V compatibility.
  IMU I2 C                                D20 SDA / D21      Short, local, twisted/ground-referenced routing only; do not
                                          SCL                reuse D20 as tach.
  IR                                      A5 through A0      Structured telemetry with age/quality; no single terrain-sensitive
                                                             sample receives stop authority.

Each ISR should read no sensor bus, allocate no memory, print no serial text, and make no motion decision. It should
increment an edge counter and capture a timestamp in volatile state. The main loop atomically snapshots those
fields and computes speed over elapsed monotonic time. Telemetry should publish pulses, window length, derived

ORBITUS ROBOTICS - FIRST MAKER FAIRE EDITION                                                                               34 / 220
BUILDING R.O.B.                                                                           COMPLETE FIELD MANUAL - ADVANCED


speed, age, saturation/overflow, and plausibility. Closed-loop tread control should begin in observation-only mode,
compare commanded with measured speed, and retain operator authority during mismatch tuning.



Successor controller study
The Arduino GIGA R1 WiFi is the leading Arduino-branded research candidate, not a drop-in replacement. Official
documentation lists 76 GPIO, 13 PWM pins, four UARTs, a dual-core STM32H747, and an FDCAN controller on
D93/D94 that still requires an external transceiver. Its I/O logic is 3.3 V with a 20 mA per-pin maximum and a 140
mA aggregate I/O limit. Every existing 5 V signal must therefore be measured and level-adapted as required.
An initial bench allocation can reserve D2/D3/D18 for three tach inputs, D4/D5/D6 for three PWM outputs, separate
ordinary GPIO for brakes/directions, D16/D17 for the actuator UART, and D93/D94 for future isolated CAN. This
allocation must pass a board-core-specific attachInterrupt(digitalPinToInterrupt(pin), ...) compile/run test,
edge-rate test, timer/PWM interaction test, and safe-reset test before a harness is designed.
The GIGA base board does not include an IMU. The GIGA Display Shield adds a BMI270 over I2 C, but a display
shield is a poor reason to keep a long noisy sensor bus. Better successors place the IMU near the controller/center-
of-motion with short SPI or I2 C wiring, isolate it from motor returns, timestamp locally, and send typed orienta-
tion/quality telemetry. The existing OAK-D Pro W also contains an IMU, but its frame, latency, USB lifecycle, and
mounting role differ from a base safety/control sensor.

   SOURCE RECORD, NOT A VERIFIED HARNESS

   The builder identifies an Arduino Mega 2560 and the repository preserves its historical Base sketch, but board
   revision, flashed hash, libraries, connector voltages, polarity, brake wiring, watchdog timing, tach interface,
   IR calibration, IMU installation, and actuator safe state were not measured for publication. The proposed
   Mega/GIGA maps remain educational comparisons. No signed hardware review exists, so pin names and
   frame fields in this chapter must not be treated as permission to connect or move the present robot.

Central coordination on the

Cerebro is a mixed Swift, Objective-C, and Objective-C++ macOS application. Its central view/controller layer coor-
dinates UI state while specialized components own serial, camera, speech, autonomy, identity, private conversation,
recording, security, and animation.

                                         dow.

Present belly compute, USB, and Ethernet record
The builder reports three Mac mini generations: Intel, M1, and the present M4. The M4 runs Cerebro. A separate
AMBER Robotics Ubuntu computer owns the vendor kinematic/core environment and the two arm CAN paths. A

The M4 presently uses two USB-C hubs plus a USB-A fan-out. Named USB loads include the Pololu stepper controller,
24-channel Maestro, Hecate USB/Bluetooth speakers, keyboard dongle, depth cameras, and the rotary/base chain

Create two inventories from the running M4: a physical port photograph with both cable ends labeled, and a

ered hubs only after grounding, backfeed, isolation, and shutdown behavior are reviewed.
Other named body loads are a conference microphone, a digitally controlled bicycle lamp whose button interface
has not been successfully automated, and a fanless pure-sine inverter recalled as 150 W continuous/300 W peak.
The lamp remains manual until its button protocol is measured and an isolated, reversible interface is designed;
never probe it from a high-current robot bus by trial and error.



Base heartbeat ownership
Cerebro evaluates controller snapshots on a timer running at about 10 Hz and treats a snapshot as stale once its
local receipt age exceeds 0.6 seconds. On the first transition from fresh to stale, it writes one neutral/braked serial
frame, stops USB refresh, releases control authority, and allows the Arduino heartbeat to expire. This is a time-based
gate, not a fixed count of three missed messages. The layered behavior is stronger than either deadline alone, but
the Arduino’s own elapsed time and physical stop result still require measurement.



ROBSerialBox as an integration history
   SOURCE TRAIL --- ANALYZING NOW

   File: Cerebro/Cerebro/ROBSerialBox.m
   Why it is here:           Read initialize_connection,   usbSerialPortPaths,   connectToDetectedBase,
   probeBaseFirmwareAtPath,     consumeBaseSerialBytes,  the    Vision  neck/torso    methods,     and


ORBITUS ROBOTICS - FIRST MAKER FAIRE EDITION                                                                                                                      39 / 220
BUILDING R.O.B.                                                                            COMPLETE FIELD MANUAL - ADVANCED



   stopBaseMotionAndDropHeartbeat together.


ROBSerialBox is the current physical-I/O façade and an archaeological record. It contains Base, Maestro, Tic, AMBER,
controller, and retired Head/Torso surfaces. At startup every POSIX descriptor receives the closed sentinel −1. Base
discovery enumerates only USB callout devices, opens candidates at 250,000 baud, pulses DTR, sends no probe
command bytes, and passively waits up to 15 seconds for the already-flashed firmware line BEGIN BASE STARTUP
SEQUENCE. Hub-dependent /dev/cu.* names therefore need not be stored. The banner identifies a firmware role; it
does not authenticate the board or verify wiring.
Serial reads are arbitrary byte chunks, not messages. Cerebro accumulates bounded data, extracts newline-
delimited records, and then interprets them. It recognizes the existing front/back IR warning sentences and a
bounded six-value numeric form when available. Legacy warning silence is reported as clearance unknown. Mae-
stro selection instead uses USB vendor/product/interface identity, while the waist Tic path uses preflighted ticcmd;
device discovery is protocol-specific rather than “first port wins.”
The next refactor should retain the existing Objective-C façade for Interface Builder while moving each descriptor,
read loop, write queue, reconnect generation, and parser into a single-owner component. Checking fd != -1 can-
not by itself make a concurrent close/write atomic. This is the practical lesson behind historical Bad file descriptor
floods.



External helpers and dependencies
The OAK-D path uses a supervised Python helper rather than loading DepthAI directly into the control process.
Cerebro can select or create a Python environment and validate the required import. Optional Amber scripts and
tools such as sshpass and ticcmd are preflighted; missing dependencies disable their feature instead of terminating
the app.

   ADVANCED CHANNEL

   Subprocess boundaries create explicit failure and update surfaces. The parent must validate executable paths,
   avoid blocking reads, bound restart backoff, authenticate local IPC, limit message sizes, and make helper un-
   availability visible. A helper crash should never preserve motion authority.



Legacy boundary
  LEGACY Historical controller data uses a fourteen-line keyed payload inside the newer authenticated transport.
New domain code should not spread that format. A narrow adapter should translate modern typed commands into
the legacy application payload until a deliberate migration replaces it.
A malformed ROBControl header or frame can be rejected before application dispatch. A well-formed, authenti-
cated envelope can still contain a malformed legacy controller payload, so the application adapter must validate
that payload separately; transport authentication does not make its contents physically safe.

Controllers and secure transport
CHAPTER 9


Control from iPhone, Watch,
and Vision Pro
  SOURCE TRAIL --- ANALYZING NOW

  File: ROBController/Consciousness/AutoNetClient/AutoNetDataTransferProtocol.swift
  Why it is here: The client control contract is the anchor.          Compare it with Cerebro’s matching
  AutoNetDataTransferProtocol.swift; the sending UI is in ConsciousViewController.mm, Watch relay in its Watch
  app directory, and spatial client under ROBControllerVision.




Controller roles
The iPhone/iPad ROBController captures manual controls and presents Map, Controls, Auto, Settings, push-to-talk,
pairing, destination autonomy, and action-approval surfaces. Its active legacy snapshot carries raw joystick coordi-
nates; it does not use the Vision controller’s normalized linear/angular representation. Apple Watch sends voice or
joystick intent through the phone and keeps no robot credential. ROBControllerVision provides a SwiftUI/Swift 6
architecture with a deterministic simulator, normalized motion, a 250 ms lease, a latched software E-stop, indepen-
dent arm control, secure Cerebro control, three H.264 feeds, and an immersive 360 view.



Explicit drive ownership
  IMPLEMENTED The latest authenticated operator to request control wins. Cerebro first stops the prior owner’s motion,
records the new owner, and broadcasts authoritative ownership. Release Control brakes ROB and returns ownership
to Cerebro. Each phone/tablet and Vision Pro needs its own pairing credential. Authentication proves the device;
ownership says which authenticated device may currently drive.
Camera subscriptions, face recognition, Messages identity, arm authority, and AI approval do not imply drive own-
ership. Test simultaneous requests, takeover under nonzero input, prior-client replay, disconnect, revocation, and
release; every transition must converge through an explicit stop.



Independent Vision arm authority
 EXPERIMENTAL / OPTIONAL The current rob-arm-control/2 path gives the left and right AMBER arms independent mea-
sured feedback, authority UUIDs, leases, selected joints, drafts, in-flight targets, and hold results. Vision never
activates an arm or changes its actuator mode. Fresh feedback (no more than 250 ms old), all-seven-joint position
mode, exact bounds, a measured baseline, and a Cerebro grant are preconditions.
On-screen lanes and paired PSVR Sense thumbsticks limit each segment to 0.08 radian and average requested
speed to 0.20 radian/s. With both arm authorities active, both vertical sticks may jog their matching selected joints
concurrently after a 0.15 dead zone. Both grips form one dead-man; releasing either asks both arms to hold. Dis-
connect, stale input/telemetry, preflight loss, target failure, scene loss, stop, disarm, or software E-stop takes the
priority-hold-both path and clears local latches. This is joint jogging, not tracked-pose IK; HIL verification remains
pending.




                                                         43
BUILDING R.O.B.                                                                                                                                   COMPLETE FIELD MANUAL - ADVANCED



ROBControl v2
  IMPLEMENTED The production control path advertises _robctl._udp, negotiates robctl/2, uses TLS 1.3 over QUIC,
pins Cerebro’s certificate, and performs a reciprocal HMAC-SHA-256 proof with a per-device 256-bit secret. Its
fixed 40-byte RCTL header carries version, kind, flags, channel, bounded payload length, monotonic sequence,
and message ID.
Validation is layered rather than identical for every message. Transport framing checks applicable bounds, role, ses-
sion, and sequence rules. Lidar telemetry adds capture-time freshness, while controller freshness is measured from
Cerebro’s local receipt time. The legacy fourteen-line application payload still requires its own decoder validation.
Pairing creates a fresh device UUID and secret for one named device. The server registry remains authoritative
for role and revocation. Bonjour advertises non-secret routing facts, not trust. Each device must receive its own
credential. The pinned server-certificate fingerprint is a public identity value rather than a secret; security depends
on protecting the device secret and private key.

Separate video service
 IMPLEMENTED  Cerebro advertises _robvideo._udp with robvideo/1. Independent front, belly, and insta360 H.264
AVCC feeds use distinct bounded pipelines. Front/belly publish up to 960 by 540; panorama uses 960 by 480;
all are capped at 20 fps and 1.5 Mbit/s on ordered reliable QUIC streams. Video has separate framing, queues,
authentication domain, and backpressure so media cannot queue in front of a stop command.
Video authorization is bound to the exact live control-session ID and authenticated controller identity; the client
opens its media stream after authentication. Control disconnect or credential revocation closes associated video.
Individual feed failure does not close control. A Vision window-to-immersive transition can retain the panorama
pipeline while independently braking motion; backgrounding tears it down.

Privacy review

The current active iPhone path requests Always location authorization and includes live latitude and longitude in
each controller snapshot. This is current privacy debt, not merely a historical format detail: remove the fields, mini-
mize their precision and retention, or document a necessary purpose and consent policy before public use. Pairing
codes, device secrets, private keys, IP addresses, SSH credentials, API keys, private logs, and location must be

metadata, but it is not a secret. Each cloud-enabled camera or microphone path needs explicit public notice and
consent handling.

   The inspected code establishes protocol intent and test fixtures, not a finished device/OS support matrix or
